Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on the FASRC cluster, add the `--gpus` flag to your `#SBATCH` directives: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH --gpus=1 ``` You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently. For example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to those devices. Inside your code, you reference them using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`). For more details, see [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/).
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on the FASRC cluster, add the `--gpus` flag to your `#SBATCH` directives: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH --gpus=1 ``` You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently. For example, to request 1 CPU and 2 GPUs: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -n 1 #SBATCH --gpus=2 ``` When you submit a GPU job, SLURM automatically assigns GPUs and restricts your job to those devices. Inside your code, you reference them using zero-based indexing (e.g., `gpu:0`, `gpu:1`). For more details, see [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster/).

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you don't specify a memory request, your SLURM job on Cannon gets a default limit of **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small. If your job uses more than 100 MB, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. You should explicitly request memory using the `--mem` flag in your `sbatch` script or `srun` command. For example: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=8G ``` This requests 8 GB of memory per node. You can use suffixes like `K`, `M`, `G`, or `T` to specify the unit [Slurm Workload Manager - srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
If you don't specify a memory request, your SLURM job on Cannon gets a default limit of **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). This is intentionally small. If your job uses more than 100 MB, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. You should explicitly request memory using the `--mem` flag in your `sbatch` script or `srun` command. For example: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=8G ``` This requests 8 GB of memory per node. You can use suffixes like `K`, `M`, `G`, or `T` to specify the unit [Slurm Workload Manager - srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).

Question 4: question_4

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The FASRC home directory has a **100 GB hard quota** and a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when you approach the limit [Home directory full – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). If you receive an email saying you are over your 100 GB quota (or a "no space left on device" error), you'll need to remove files to get back under quota. You can check your current usage with the `quota` command: ```bash quota $HOME ``` See [Checking quota and usage – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

If you receive an email saying you are over your 100 GB quota (or a "no space left on device" error), you'll need to remove files to get back under quota. Question 5: question_5

❓ Question

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the retrieved documentation, here is the information for **Lab directories** (specifically the complimentary `holylabs` offering): * **Storage Size:** 4 TB (this cannot be expanded) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) * **File Count:** The documentation does not specify a limit on the number of files. * **Path:** `/n/holylabs` * **Retention:** Allowed (indefinite) * **Backup:** No backup (no snapshots) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) For comparison, **Home directories** (individual user accounts) have a 100 GB hard limit and daily snapshots, but are not intended for large-scale data storage [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage). If you need more storage, FASRC offers paid options like **Compute Storage** and **Lab Storage**, as well as the complimentary **netscratch** offering (50 TB, but with a 90-day retention policy) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.

Lab directories reside under /n/holylabs.

Question 7: question_7

❓ Question

What hostname do I SSH to for command-line access to the FASRC cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-from-abroad

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
SSH to **`login.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** using your FASRC username. For example: ```bash ssh your_username@login.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` This connects you to the FASRC login nodes, which are shared, multi-user resources intended for light tasks like editing files, checking job status, and launching interactive sessions or batch jobs — not for running heavy computations directly [Command line access with Terminal (login nodes)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access/).
✅ Expected Answer
SSH to login.rc.fas.harvard.edu using your FASRC username, password, and OpenAuth token.

Question 9: question_9

❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-stats, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise3/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/wckey.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On FASRC, you can check your lab's fairshare information using the `sshare` command. Here are a few useful variations: - **For a specific lab account:** ```bash sshare -a --account=<labname> ``` This shows a list of users from that lab who have been active in the last six months along with their fairshare information [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/). - **For your own account:** ```bash sshare ``` This will show your personal fairshare score. - **To see all your Slurm account associations:** ```bash sacctmgr show associations where user=<username> ``` Or in parsable format: ```bash sacctmgr -p show assoc format=cluster,account,user,qos,priority | grep <labname> ``` Fairshare scores determine job priority on the cluster — labs that haven't used their full resource grant get higher priority for their jobs [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account=<lab> -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The home directories have daily snapshots stored in a hidden `.snapshot` directory. You can access these snapshots by explicitly navigating to `.snapshot` (e.g., `ls .snapshot`), where you'll find hourly, daily, and weekly snapshot subdirectories. To restore a file, simply copy it from the appropriate snapshot subdirectory. **Important notes:** * If you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are **not recoverable**. * The home directory has a 100 GB hard limit and is not suitable for I/O-intensive jobs or large datasets.
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here is how to find out how much memory your job used and how to request more for next time: ### 1. Find out how much memory your job used * **Use `jobstats`**: Run `jobstats <JOBID>` to see the memory usage of a completed job. * **Important**: This only gives an accurate peak memory measurement if the job **completed successfully**. If the job was killed for running out of memory (`OUT_OF_MEMORY`), the recorded memory usage will be inaccurate [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). * **Check job state**: You can confirm a job was killed for memory issues by checking its state with `sacct -j <JOBID>`. If it shows `OUT_OF_ME+` (or similar) in the State column, it was terminated due to exceeding the memory limit [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). ### 2. Request more memory for next time Add the `--mem` directive to your SLURM submission script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` * Replace `4G` with the amount of memory you need. You can specify the amount in MB (e.g., `--mem 4000`) or GB (e.g., `--mem 4G`) [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). * **Tip**: Start with a reasonably large value (job slots average ~4000 MB per core), then use `jobstats` on a successful run to see what you actually used. Set your next request slightly higher than what `jobstats` reports, since `--mem` is a hard limit [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). ### Summary of steps 1. Run a test job with a high `--mem` request (e.g., `--mem 8G`). 2. Once it completes successfully, run `jobstats <JOBID>` to see the peak memory used. 3. In your next submission script, set `--mem` to a value slightly above what `jobstats` reported.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=<N>G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the two-factor code for logging into FASRC is **not** the same as your Harvard Key code. FASRC uses its own separate OpenAuth two-factor authentication system. However, if you already have the **Duo Mobile** app installed on your phone for your Harvard Key, you can add your FASRC token to the same app so you can generate both codes in one place. ### How to set up Duo Mobile for FASRC 1. **Get the QR code:** - From a computer or tablet (not your phone), visit [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your FASRC username and password. - You'll see a page explaining that a link has been sent via email. - Open the email and click the link (or copy/paste it into your browser). - This will display a QR code on the screen. Leave this page open. 2. **Add the token to Duo Mobile:** - Open the Duo Mobile app on your phone. - Tap the **+** icon to add a new token. - Use your phone's camera to scan the QR code displayed on your computer screen. - Duo will recognize the code and add the FASRC token to your app. 3. **Use the code:** - You'll now see two entries in Duo Mobile: one for Harvard Key and one for FASRC. - Tap the key icon next to the FASRC entry to display your 6-digit OpenAuth code. - Use this code to log into the cluster, connect to the RC VPN, etc. For more details, see the full guide: [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/)
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To find available versions and load a specific one on Cannon: ### 1. Find available versions Run `module avail <package>` (e.g., `module avail matlab`) to list the versions of that package that are available in your current environment. For a broader view that isn't limited by currently loaded modules, use `module --default avail` or `module spider <package>` [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). ### 2. Load a specific version Always specify the full module name, including the version, when loading: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 ``` This ensures your workflow uses the exact version you tested, regardless of whether the default version changes later. ### 3. Why this matters If you only run `module load matlab`, the system will load the default version, which may change over time. FASRC **strongly recommends** specifying the full name and version to protect your reproducibility [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/).
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider <name> to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load <name>/<version>) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/gReLU/grelu.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a **virtual environment**. Direct use of `pip` outside of a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters. Here’s the recommended workflow: 1. **Start an interactive session** (required for creating environments): ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 2. **Load the Python module** (which includes Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 3. **Create an environment** (default location is `~/.conda/envs`): ```bash mamba create -n my_project_env ``` Or create and install packages in one step: ```bash mamba create -n my_project_env python=3.10 numpy pandas ``` 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate my_project_env ``` 5. **Install additional packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y package_name ``` 6. **Deactivate** when done: ```bash source deactivate ``` **Important notes:** - The FASRC `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel, since `repo.anaconda.com` is blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation). - If you want to store packages in a different location (e.g., a Lab shared directory), set `CONDA_PKGS_DIRS` and `CONDA_ENVS_PATH` before creating the environment.
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mcs.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### How Job Priority is Determined on Cannon On Cannon, job priority is calculated using Slurm's **Multifactor Priority Plugin**, which combines two main components: 1. **FairShare Score** – Your account's FairShare score (based on how much of your allocated share you've actually used) is multiplied by a weighting factor of **10,000,000** to produce an integer priority value. A FairShare of 1.0 yields a priority of 10,000,000; a FairShare of 0.5 yields 5,000,000. This component evolves dynamically as your account's usage changes over time [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). 2. **Job Age** – Jobs accrue additional priority over time the longer they remain in the pending queue. This ensures that jobs waiting longer eventually get scheduled [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). Additional factors from the Multifactor Priority Plugin can also influence priority, including: - **Job Size Factor** – Larger jobs (relative to system size) receive higher priority. - **Nice Factor** – Users can adjust priority via the `--nice` flag (positive values lower priority; negative values raise it, but only privileged users can specify negative values). - **Partition Factor** – Each partition has an assigned priority tier; jobs in higher-tier partitions are dispatched before those in lower-tier partitions. ### How to View Your Pending Jobs' Priority You can view the priority of your pending jobs using `squeue` with the `P` (priority) format specifier: ```bash squeue -u <your_username> -o "%.10i %.10P %.10j %t %R" ``` This will display your job ID, priority (`P`), job name, state, and nodes. You can also view priority for all pending jobs: ```bash squeue -o "%.10i %.10P %.10j %.10t %.10R" ``` For more details on available `squeue` output formats, see the Slurm documentation on [squeue](https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html).
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p <partition> to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what I can tell you: ### Should you use Globus? **Yes, Globus is a good choice for large dataset transfers**, especially if: - You're transferring data from external collaborators who don't have FASRC accounts - You need to transfer very large files or many files - You want a more robust, resumable transfer mechanism than SCP Globus is a third-party service that FASRC uses for file sharing and transfers [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). It's an alternative to SSH/SCP specifically designed for larger or more complex transfers. ### Can you transfer directly to your home directory? The documentation doesn't explicitly address home directory transfer restrictions, but here's what I can share about FASRC storage: - **Home directories** are accessible via SMB mount, SCP, SFTP, and Globus [Mounting Storage on Desktop or Laptop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage/) - **Scratch storage** (`/n/nestscratch`) and **holylabs** (`/n/holylabs`) also support Globus transfers [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) ### Recommendation for large datasets For **very large datasets**, consider: 1. **Transfer to scratch or holylabs first** rather than home directory, as these have much larger capacities (50TB for netscratch, 4TB for holylabs) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) 2. Home directories typically have strict size limits and are not intended for large data storage Would you like help setting up Globus transfers or learning more about the different storage options available?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.

Question 17: question_17

❓ Question

I accidentally deleted some files in my home directory on Cannon. Can I get them back, and is there any situation where I can't?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rdiff-backup, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, you can usually get them back—**but only if you haven't deleted your entire home directory**. Here's how it works: - **Home directories are backed up** with daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks ([Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/)). - Snapshots are stored in a hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home. You can access them by explicitly `cd`-ing into `.snapshot` (it won't show up in normal `ls` output). - To restore a deleted file, simply copy it from the appropriate date-stamped subdirectory inside `.snapshot`. ### When you **can't** recover files: - **If you delete your entire home directory**, the snapshots are also deleted, and this is **not recoverable** ([Home and Lab directories](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/)). - If the file was deleted more than 2 weeks ago, the relevant snapshot may have already been pruned. ### What to do now: 1. Log in to Cannon. 2. Check what snapshots are available: ```bash ls -la ~/.snapshot/ ``` 3. Navigate into the most recent snapshot that still contains your file and copy it back: ```bash cp ~/.snapshot/<date>/path/to/your/file ~ ``` If you've deleted your entire home directory, contact FASRC support immediately—there may be no way to recover the data.
✅ Expected Answer
Usually yes: home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, so you can recover recently deleted files from the .snapshot directory. The exception is if you delete your entire home directory — that also deletes the snapshots, and it is not recoverable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Question 18: question_18

❓ Question

I want to run a short GPU job on Cannon. How do I request the GPU and make sure it has enough memory so it isn't killed?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/cgroup.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/cgroup.conf.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/.github/workflows/Languages-Python-Example1.yml, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Parallel_HDF5/Example2/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/Recipe3/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/AITools/ollama.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to request a GPU on Cannon and avoid memory-related job kills: ### 1. Request the GPU Use the `--gres=gpu:N` flag in your SLURM script (or interactive `salloc`/`srun` command), where `N` is the number of GPUs you need. For a single GPU: ```bash #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 ``` You'll also need to specify the GPU partition (e.g., `gpu` or `gpu_test`) and a time limit: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu_test #SBATCH -t 0-01:00 # 1 hour ``` ### 2. Request Enough Memory By default, SLURM on FASRC clusters imposes a very small memory limit (100 MB per node) [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). If your job exceeds this, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` state. You can request memory in two ways: - **Per-node memory:** `--mem=8000` (8 GB total for the node) - **Per-CPU memory:** `--mem-per-cpu=4G` (4 GB per allocated core) For example, a typical short GPU job might look like: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH -p gpu_test #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 #SBATCH -t 0-01:00 #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu=4G #SBATCH -c 4 #SBATCH -J my_gpu_job #SBATCH -o slurm.%j.out #SBATCH -e slurm.%j.err module load cuda/12.1.1 # ... your GPU code here ... ``` ### Key points to avoid being killed: - **Always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`** — the default 100 MB is almost certainly too small [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). - **Check GPU memory usage** inside your job (e.g., `nvidia-smi`) to ensure your code isn't exceeding the GPU's VRAM, which can also cause out-of-memory errors. - **Use the `gpu_test` partition** for short, experimental jobs, or the `gpu` partition for production workloads. Would you like help tailoring a specific SLURM script for your workload?
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=<N>G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC Account Qualifications documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply: 1. **A supported affiliation** — just like faculty, they need an affiliation that FASRC recognizes. 2. **Their own 33-digit billing string(s)** — this is the key difference. Ordinary researchers (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, etc.) are sponsored by a PI and do not need their own billing string. Non-faculty PIs, however, must provide their own billing information because they are responsible for charges to their own cluster group. As the documentation states: > "If you have a non-faculty appointment but have PI rights with a supported affiliation and your own 33-digit billing string(s), you should select NON-FACULTY PI (your account and PI rights will need to be vetted before approval. FASRC may contact you for more information)." [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) Additionally, their account and PI rights will be vetted before approval, and FASRC may contact them for more information.
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Question 24: question_24

❓ Question

Our lab has grant money and wants to buy extra compute nodes for the cluster. Can we just have them racked whenever we're ready?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you cannot simply have nodes racked whenever you're ready. According to FASRC documentation, purchasing hardware for the cluster is a coordinated process that takes time for hardware to be built and installed [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). However, there is a mechanism for labs to purchase hardware: - **Fairshare system**: Labs can purchase hardware for the cluster, which adds to their "Share" in Slurm's fairshare system. This increases your lab's priority for job scheduling [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). - **Process**: You need to **contact FASRC directly** to arrange hardware purchases. They coordinate the procurement, build, and installation process [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). **Next steps:** 1. Contact FASRC at [https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) to discuss purchasing hardware for the cluster 2. Once hardware arrives, your lab's fairshare priority will increase commensurately Would you like help finding the right contact person or more details about how the fairshare system works?
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC will discuss your computing needs, coordinate a quote, and install the new hardware, but as of the 2019 cluster refresh and the move to water-cooling, new nodes can be added into the cluster only at certain times of the year.

Question 25: question_25

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the retrieved documentation, I can provide some general information, but the specific details about Chan School billing for outside collaborators and what happens when a PI leaves are not fully covered in the indexed sources. ## What we know from the documentation: **Account sponsorship:** According to the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/), a Chan School user's PI must have an existing FASRC account to sponsor/approve an account request. The PI receives an email with a link to approve the account. **External users:** The documentation notes that if you sign up as an external user, your request must be "manually vetted" and may take slightly longer to process. **General policy:** The [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) page states that cluster access is intended only for legitimate research purposes and references the FASRC [Acceptable Use](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/) guidelines. ## What's not covered: The indexed documentation does **not** appear to contain specific answers to: 1. **Who pays** for an outside collaborator's compute time when sponsored by a Chan School PI 2. **What happens to access** if the sponsoring PI leaves These are important policy questions that would likely be addressed in Chan School-specific billing agreements or FASRC's account management policies. I'd recommend: - Contacting the [HCSPH Bioinformatics Core](http://bioinformatics.sph.harvard.edu/) for Chan School-specific billing policies - Reaching out to FASRC support directly via their [ticketing system](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/support/) for questions about account access when sponsors depart - Checking with your Chan School department administrator about any specific billing arrangements for outside collaborators
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Question 26: question_26

❓ Question

How long can a job run on the Chan School's own GPU nodes, and what GPUs are actually in them?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise4/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-information-on-storage-and-use-of-confidential-data-hrci

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The Chan School (HSPH) documentation doesn't appear to describe dedicated Chan School GPU nodes with their own time limits or GPU hardware. Instead, HSPH researchers use the **shared FAS Research Computing cluster** and its standard public GPU partitions. To find the actual GPU models and time limits, you'd need to check the public partition details: 1. Run `spart` to see which GPU partitions you have access to. 2. Run `scontrol show partition <partition_name>` to see the time limit and node specs for each partition. 3. Run `scontrol show node <nodename>` to see the GPU model on a specific node. The GPU partitions on the FASRC cluster (Cannon) are documented at [Running Jobs – Slurm partitions](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/#Slurm_partitions). If you have a specific partition name (e.g., `gpu`, `gpu_p100`, etc.), let me know and I can look up its details.
✅ Expected Answer
The hsph_gpu partition has a 3 day time limit. The block contains 128 AMD Genoa cores and 8 Nvidia H200 GPUs; each node is water-cooled with 64 cores, 1.5TB of RAM, and 4 GPUs, and the nodes are interconnected with NDR Infiniband. (The CPU-only hsph partition also has a 3 day time limit.)

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Question 27: question_27

❓ Question

Do Harvard Chan people still get charged that yearly per-person fee just to have a Research Computing account?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-information-on-storage-and-use-of-confidential-data-hrci

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, the billing model for Harvard Chan School (HSPH) users is **billed at the school level**, not as a per-person fee charged to individual researchers. Specifically, the [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) page states: > **HCSPH** – Research Computing has an arrangement with the Harvard T. H. Chan School of Public Health. Cluster and/or storage usage for accounts for faculty, staff, researchers and other appointed user are **billed to HCSPH at the school level**. This means the school pays for access as a whole, and individual researchers do not pay a yearly per-person fee to have an account. However, the document notes that "Cluster usage and additional resources may be subject to charges to the PI, school, or department," and "All billing is done via Harvard internal billing codes at the Tub/school level." For more details, you can refer to the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/) and the [HCSPH & FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is no longer used as of fiscal year 2019. Standard services — including accounts and access to the FASRC high-performance computing cluster — are effectively free to Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI, because these FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you can't just copy-paste the Cannon Remote-SSH setup to FASSE. The two clusters have different architectures and login nodes, so the SSH proxy command needs to be adapted. Here's what changes for FASSE: 1. **Login node**: Cannon uses `cannon` as the bastion/login host; FASSE uses `fasselogin01` (or `fasselogin02`). You'll need to update your SSH config to point to the FASSE login node instead. 2. **SLURM job allocation**: The `salloc` command in the ProxyCommand must be adjusted for FASSE's partitions, QoS, and resource limits. For example, you might need to change `--partition gpu_test` to a partition available on FASSE, and adjust `--mem`, `--time`, etc. 3. **Node naming**: The `nc $SLURM_NODELIST 22` part works the same way, but the compute node hostnames will be different on FASSE. **Recommended approach for FASSE:** - Use the same two approaches documented for Cannon: - **Approach I (Remote-SSH)**: Modify the ProxyCommand in your `~/.ssh/config` to use the FASSE login node and appropriate SLURM directives for FASSE. - **Approach II (Remote Tunnel)**: This method is cluster-agnostic and may be simpler — you download the VS Code CLI on the compute node and create a tunnel interactively. For detailed, cluster-specific steps, see the official guide: [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). Would you like help adapting the SSH config for FASSE, or would you prefer to try the Remote Tunnel approach instead?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid

🤖 Assistant Message #2

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, slurm.schedmd.com/containers.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC doesn't run a Docker daemon on the cluster — it provides **Podman** instead. To get the VS Code Dev Containers extension to work, you need to: 1. **Start a Podman system service** on your compute node (in a VS Code terminal on the cluster): ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This creates a socket at `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock`. 2. **Update your `devcontainer.json`** to bind-mount that socket and point Dev Containers at it. A minimal configuration looks like: ```json { "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-<UID>/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" }, "workspaceFolder": "${localWorkspaceFolder}", "workspaceMount": "source=${localWorkspaceFolder},target=${workspaceFolder},type=bind" } ``` Replace `<UID>` with your actual numeric user ID (e.g., `1234`). 3. **Set the Docker socket path** so Dev Containers knows where to find the container runtime. You can do this by setting the `DOCKER_HOST` environment variable: ```bash export DOCKER_HOST=unix:///var/run/docker-host.sock ``` (This works because the socket is bind-mounted to `/var/run/docker-host.sock` inside the container.) ### Why this works - Podman is daemonless and doesn't have a persistent background daemon like Docker, so you start a temporary API service on-demand [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). - The Dev Containers extension expects a Docker-compatible socket; by exposing the Podman socket through a bind mount, you give it a compatible interface without needing a real Docker daemon [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). ### Troubleshooting - If you get permission errors, wrap your container commands in `unshare -r` to map your UID/GID to root inside the namespace: ```bash unshare -r # run your container commands here exit # leave the namespace when done ``` [Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/) - Make sure the `podman system service` is still running when Dev Containers tries to build or run containers. If it times out, restart it.
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-<uid>/podman/podman.sock', replacing <uid> with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rstudio-server-vs-rstudio-desktop, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to set the `KERAS_BACKEND` environment variable to `tensorflow`: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` This is required for Keras 3.0 and above, which is bundled with TensorFlow 2.16.1+ [TensorFlow – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/). If you want this to be set automatically every time you activate your conda environment, you can add it to the conda activation script: ```bash echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` Then reactivate your environment with `source activate tf2.16.1_cuda12.1`.
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the card into smaller instances. To use a full GPU instance, you need to explicitly tell PyTorch which MIG device to use by setting the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable. Here's what to do: 1. **Find the MIG device name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set the environment variable to the MIG instance:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d ``` (Replace with the actual MIG ID from your `nvidia-smi -L` output.) 3. **Or automate it:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This step is **not necessary** on the `gpu` partition, only on `gpu_test` where MIG mode is active. See the FASRC PyTorch documentation for more details: [PyTorch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/)
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname that the CryoSPARC master binds to is set in the **`config.sh`** file inside your `cryosparc_master` directory: ``` $INSTALL_DIR/cryosparc_master/config.sh ``` Look for the line: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` Change `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the correct hostname of the node where the master is running, then restart the master: ```bash cryosparcm restart ``` If you also changed the port number, you'll need to run `cryosparcm fixdbport` before restarting, as noted in the FASRC documentation [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md).
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

Running `cryosparcm start` brings up the following six applications: * **app** (CLI) * **command_core** * **command_rtp** * **command_vis** * **database** (MongoDB) * **webapp** These components communicate with one another over TCP, with their ports determined by a configurable base port set in `cryosparc_master/config.sh` [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/).

Running `cryosparcm start` brings up six applications: app (cli), command_core, command_rtp, command_vis, database (MongoDB), and webapp. At the top level cryosparcm is a Supervisor-based shell script that manages them, and they mostly communicate with one another over TCP.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No — don't leave it in netscratch.** FASRC documentation explicitly states: > Place `file.bin` in a **lab share** (do not put in netscratch) — this will be the location of your `--model_dir`. ### What to do: 1. **Unpack the weights** on a login or compute node: ```bash unzstd file_name.bin.zst ``` This produces `file_name.bin`. 2. **Move it to your lab's shared storage** (e.g., `/n/holylabs/LABS/<lab_name>/...`). The example script uses a path like: ``` /n/holylabs/LABS/jharvard_lab/Lab/alphafold3/model_parameters ``` 3. **Point your `--model_dir`** argument to that lab-share location when running AlphaFold 3. The databases themselves are already available cluster-wide at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3`, so you don't need to download those. The model parameters are the only user-specific file you need to place in your lab share.
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/JobArrays/Exercise_nonsequential_maxarray/array_script.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to determine the default resource allocations (partition, memory, time) for a SLURM job when these parameters are omitted from the `#SBATCH` directives in an `sbatch` script. It performed multiple vector store searches to find specific cluster documentation (specifically referencing FASRC/Harvard resources) to identify the fallback values. **Summary of Attempt:** The agent searched for SLURM default behaviors regarding missing partition, memory, and time constraints. The retrieved documents provided general SLURM state definitions (PENDING, RUNNING, etc.) and examples of script structures but did not explicitly list the numerical defaults for memory or time for the specific cluster environment in the visible snippets. **Answer:** When you omit `#SBATCH --partition`, `#SBATCH --mem`, and `#SBATCH --time` from your script, the SLURM scheduler applies the **default values** configured for the cluster. While the exact numbers depend on your specific cluster's configuration (e.g., FASRC, HPC, etc.), here is the general behavior: 1. **Partition:** If no partition is specified, SLURM typically defaults to a partition named **`default`** (or sometimes `batch` or `all`, depending on the site configuration). If a `default` partition does not exist, the job may be rejected or placed in a system-defined default queue. 2. **Memory:** If no memory limit is specified, the scheduler usually assigns a **default memory limit per node** (e.g., 2GB, 4GB, or 8GB) or allows the job to request as much as is available on the node, subject to the partition's overall limits. However, it is safer to assume a modest default (like 1GB or 2GB) unless documented otherwise. 3. **Time:** If no time limit is specified, the job is typically assigned the **maximum time limit** allowed for the default partition (e.g., 24 hours, 7 days, or 1 week). This prevents jobs from running indefinitely but allows them to run for a long duration. **Important Note:** The run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50. For precise values, you should check your cluster's documentation or run `sinfo` (to see partition defaults) and `scontrol show partition <partition_name>` (to see specific default limits for memory and time).
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/overview.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/Job_Arrays/job_arrays_1/test.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/MATLAB/Example5/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/rosetta.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can use a SLURM job array for this, but **each array element counts as an independent job** for scheduling and queue limits. ### How it works - Submit with `--array=0-11999` (or `--array=1-12000`) in your `#SBATCH` line. - Each element runs the same script but with a different value of the environment variable `$SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID`, which you can use to select inputs, set output filenames, etc. - See the [Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/) guide for details on array variables and examples. ### Does it eat into your job limit? **Yes.** As the FASRC documentation states, *"Each array element is treated by the scheduler as an independent job for the sake of scheduling"* [Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). That means: - All 12,000 elements count toward your **queued/pending job limit**. - They also count toward your **running job limit**. ### Practical advice for 12,000 jobs The [Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/) guide recommends: - **Pause 0.5–1 second** between `sbatch` submissions (if submitting individually) to avoid overwhelming the scheduler. - Ensure each job takes **at least 5–10 minutes** so the scheduler has time to set up and schedule them. - **Avoid email notifications** except for errors — jobs that overwhelm the email queue will be killed. - Consider using **GNU parallel** or TACC's launcher, which are installed on the cluster, for high-throughput workloads. ### A note on array size limits Slurm's default `MaxArraySize` is 1001, but can go up to 4,000,001 depending on the cluster configuration [Slurm Job Array Support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html). If 12,000 exceeds your cluster's configured limit, you may need to split into multiple arrays or contact FASRC support. Would you like help writing the batch script with `$SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID` to handle your 12,000 inputs?
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your job takes about 3 hours on the shared partition, you should request **9 hours** for the `serial_requeue` partition. The `serial_requeue` partition contains a mix of hardware, and your job may end up on a substantially slower host. A good rule of thumb is to allow for a factor of three variance in speed when estimating runtime for this partition [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/). So, use `#SBATCH --time=09:00:00` (or `--time=9:00:00`) in your SLURM submission script.
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

🤖 Assistant Message #2

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The short answer is: **Fairshare is a global quantity, not a per-partition one.** Even if you only run on your lab’s private partition, your account is still charged for those jobs against the cluster-wide fairshare pool. Here’s why: 1. **Fairshare is global.** As the documentation explains, *"We give RawShares to everyone that can be used anywhere on the cluster since Fairshare is a global quantity. Hence a user is charged regardless of what partition they use."* [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) 2. **Private partitions get RawShares, but they don't exempt you.** Groups with private partitions are granted *RawShares* equivalent to the hardware in that partition, but those shares are still part of the global fairshare calculation. Your usage on the private partition still counts toward your account's overall fairshare score. 3. **Your fairshare can still drop.** If other groups in your cluster are using more resources relative to their shares, your fairshare percentage can decrease — even if you're only using your own hardware. Fairshare is relative to the entire cluster's usage patterns. **What you can do:** - Once your purchased hardware arrives and is installed, your RawShares will be increased, and your fairshare should improve commensurately. [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) - If you want to understand your current fairshare or discuss options, you can contact FASRC directly. [Contact FASRC](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/about/contact) In short: buying nodes gives you dedicated hardware, but fairshare is still a cluster-wide accounting system that tracks relative usage across all groups.
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

[Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/) - If you want to understand your current fairshare or discuss options, you can contact FASRC directly. docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/bhi-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor3.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jobstats

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I can provide some general guidance, but I should note that the specific details about how FASRC's compute score (fairshare) system handles node-failure jobs and cancelled jobs are not fully covered in the retrieved sources. Here's what I can say from the documentation: **Node-failure jobs:** When a job dies from a node failure, SLURM typically cancels it with the message `JOB <jobid> CANCELLED AT <time> DUE TO NODE FAILURE` [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). These jobs are automatically requeued by the scheduler. However, the documentation doesn't explicitly state whether compute time consumed before the node failure counts against your lab's fairshare score. **Cancelled jobs:** Jobs you manually cancel (via `scancel`) or that are cancelled for other reasons are marked as `CANCELLED` [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). Again, the documentation doesn't specify whether the compute time used before cancellation counts against your score. **Fairshare scoring:** The fairshare system uses a decay function where your "score" decreases over time as other users consume resources, and increases when your lab's share of usage is below its fairshare proportion [Fairshare – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). The score is used to prioritize job scheduling, not as a billing mechanism. **What I can't answer from the documentation:** - Whether compute time from node-failure jobs counts against your fairshare score - Whether compute time from manually cancelled jobs counts - The specific time window or decay rate for how long wasted compute affects your score **Recommendation:** I'd suggest contacting [RCHelp](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) directly with details about the specific jobs and node failures. They can clarify FASRC's specific policy on whether and how node failures and cancelled jobs impact your lab's fairshare score, and provide the exact timeline for when any impact would decay.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 42: question_42

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/other-institutions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, **no, you cannot be added to the Nielsen agreement**. According to the [Nielsen Dataset documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset/), the Master Access Agreement explicitly states: > "Data and License is made solely to the individual researcher via Nielsen site. This research can be Ph.D. student or postdoc that has a tenure-track faculty registered on the site. **Data is not available to undergraduates or masters students.**" Since you're a master's student, you're not eligible for direct access to the Nielsen consumer panel data under the current Harvard agreement. Your advisor (a tenure-track faculty member) would need to be the registered researcher on the Nielsen site, but even then, only PhD students and postdocs working under their supervision can be granted access. If you need to work with this data, you might consider: - Collaborating with a PhD student or postdoc in your advisor's lab who has access - Checking if your advisor has alternative data sources that include master's students - Consulting with your advisor about whether there are other licensed datasets available to you
✅ Expected Answer
No. Under the Master Access Agreement, the Nielsen data and license are made solely to the individual researcher registered via the Chicago Booth Nielsen site. That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.
