Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. According to FASRC's account qualifications: - **If she is a faculty member at another university** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should choose **EXTERNAL** status during sign-up. - **If she is a student or researcher at another university or company** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also choose **EXTERNAL** status. She will need to be sponsored/authorized by a Harvard PI or management to get the account approved. ### Ongoing requirements to keep her access active: 1. **She must remain subscribed to the FASRC notifications mailing list** — this is a requirement for all users. 2. **She must continue to use the account for legitimate research purposes** that benefit Harvard University's research projects. 3. **She is held accountable** by Harvard University's Electronic Access and Information Security policies. The Harvard PI sponsoring her account is responsible for ensuring her access remains appropriate. If you'd like more details on the account request process, you can find it at [Request an FAS RC Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Question 73: question_73

❓ Question

I'm a new PI with a HarvardKey but the system won't let me approve my grad student's FASRC account request. What do I need to do?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
It sounds like your PI profile isn't yet recognized in the FASRC portal, so the system can't link you to your grad student's request. Here's what to do: 1. **Confirm your PI status in the portal** - Log in to the [FAS RC & Informatics Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) with your HarvardKey. - Go to the **PI Management** section and verify that your lab group(s) and affiliations are listed. If they're missing or incorrect, you'll need to add or update them there. 2. **Ensure your PI account is fully vetted** - If you selected **NON-FACULTY PI** during signup (or if your faculty appointment isn't yet reflected), your account and PI rights require vetting by FASRC staff before you can approve requests. FASRC may contact you for additional information. 3. **Check your grad student's request** - Once your PI profile is active, your grad student's request should appear in your portal dashboard for approval. Make sure they selected you as their PI/sponsor when they submitted their request. 4. **If it still doesn't work** - Contact FASRC directly for help updating your PI profile or resolving the approval issue. For more details, see the [Cluster Account Signup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup/) and [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) pages.
✅ Expected Answer
Complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers at https://onboard.rc.fas.harvard.edu/onboard/. The onboarding checklist states that if you have a HarvardKey but are denied access to approve new accounts, you should visit and complete the FAS Onboard tool for approvers.

❓ Question

We're wrapping up a project and want to push the raw data off to tape. Before we start, what constraints are there on how the data has to be laid out, and what does it cost per year?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about tape storage: ## Constraints on Data Layout 1. **Tar archives recommended** — You should create `tar` archives of your data rather than copying raw files directly. The documentation recommends breaking large datasets into smaller archives (e.g., 50–200 GB each) to reduce the risk of total data loss from corruption [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). 2. **File count limits** — Tape allocations have file count limits based on size. For example, a 200 TB allocation has a default limit of 2,000,000 files (200 TB / 100 MB) [Starfish Zones Data Visualization Tool](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management/). 3. **Single directory requirement** — If using `tar`, your data should be contained within the same directory; the archiving approach assumes a single directory tree [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). 4. **Checksums recommended** — Create checksums (e.g., SHA-1) of your archives to verify integrity when retrieving data later [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). 5. **Access via Globus only** — Tape storage cannot be mounted or accessed via SSH/SCP/SFTP; it is only accessible via Globus [Mounting Storage on Desktop or Laptop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage/). 6. **Permissions** — Only the PI and General Manager have default access to tape via Globus; they must explicitly share access with other lab members [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). ## Cost The documentation references that tape storage is a paid offering managed through the Storage Service Center and allocated via Coldfront, but the specific per-TiB annual rate for tape storage is not provided in the retrieved documents. You can request a tape allocation through the [Coldfront Allocation Dashboard](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/coldfront-allocation-management/) and contact the Storage Service Center for current pricing [Storage Modernization Initiative: FAQs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs/). For billing details, charges are based on allocation size (TiB) and storage offering, billed monthly based on annual rates [Data Storage Billing](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing/). Would you like help with the next steps, such as requesting a tape allocation or preparing your data for archiving?
✅ Expected Answer
Tape (NESE) storage is sold in 20TB increments, allows ten thousand files per folder, and requires file sizes between 1GiB and 100GiB. It costs $15/yr per TB, is externally managed by the Northeast Storage Exchange (NESE), and data is transferred to tape using Globus. It is intended for long-term storage of inactive research data after project completion or for data retention purposes.

Question 75: question_75

❓ Question

I put a dataset in the Everyone folder so another lab could grab it, but it never shows up in Globus. Is Globus broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus isn't broken—files placed in the **Everyone** folder simply **do not sync to Globus**. According to FASRC documentation, the Everyone directory is "intended only for internal sharing" and is explicitly **not available on Globus** [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). Globus only exposes the **Lab** folder (and other designated shared collections) for external collaboration. If you want another lab to access your data via Globus, you'll need to move or copy the dataset into your lab's Globus-shared directory instead.
✅ Expected Answer
No, that is expected behavior. The Everyone subdirectory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended only for internal sharing with other labs on the cluster — it is not available on Globus. The Lab subdirectory is the one shared out via Globus, so data that has to move over Globus needs to live under Lab.

❓ Question

Is there already a samtools container sitting on the cluster somewhere, or do I have to pull one from Docker Hub myself?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG15/shifter.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/macaulay2, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, there is no pre-installed samtools container on the FASRC cluster. You will need to pull/build one yourself. Here are your options: 1. **Pull from Docker Hub and convert to Singularity:** You can build a Singularity image from an OCI container image located in Docker Hub or another registry using `singularity build` [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/). 2. **Download an existing Singularity image:** You can download an existing Singularity image from the SingularityCE Container Library or another image repository [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster/). For running containers on the cluster, Singularity is recommended for ease of use in an HPC environment [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/). Would you like help with the specific commands to pull and convert a samtools container?
✅ Expected Answer
There is one already on the cluster. Cluster nodes automount a CernVM-File System at /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/, which provides Singularity images for the BioContainers project (auto-generated from Bioconda packages). Images follow the layout /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/FIRST_LETTER/SECOND_LETTER/PACKAGE_NAME:VERSION--CONDA_BUILD, for example: `singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help`. Expect a 10-30 second delay the first time the path is accessed on a compute node that has not mounted/cached it.

Question 77: question_77

❓ Question

A spack install blew up and by the time I looked the build log was already gone. How do I stop spack from throwing the logs away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Spack automatically deletes build logs after a successful install, but you can prevent that with the `--keep-build-tree` flag or by setting a config option. **Quick fix for a single install:** ```bash spack install --keep-build-tree <package> ``` **Permanent fix via config:** Add this to your `~/.spack/config.yaml` (or the site-wide config): ```yaml config: build_logs_root: /path/to/keep/logs install_keep_tree: true ``` - `install_keep_tree: true` tells Spack not to remove the build tree (and its logs) after a successful install. - `build_logs_root` lets you point logs to a specific directory if you want them organized separately. You can also find logs for a *failed* install in `~/.spack/logs/` — Spack only deletes logs on **success**, so if the install actually failed, the logs should still be there. Let me know if you'd like help locating your existing logs or setting up the config file.
✅ Expected Answer
Spack builds in a `stage` directory located in /tmp and cleans that space up once it finishes building, whether the build succeeded or failed — which is why the logs disappear. To keep them, first set the `$TMP` environment variable to a location you want the stage files dumped into, then add the `--keep-stage` flag, e.g. `spack install --keep-stage <package>`, which tells spack to keep the staging files rather than remove them.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Question 78: question_78

❓ Question

Software I built with spack runs fine on the node I built it on but won't run on nodes in other partitions. How do I build something that works everywhere on the cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue you're experiencing is likely due to **architecture-specific optimizations** in your Spack build. When Spack compiles software, it may target the specific CPU architecture of the node where you built it, which can cause binaries to fail on nodes with different CPU types or microarchitectures. To build software that works across all nodes on the cluster, you should: 1. **Use a portable target architecture** when building with Spack. Instead of targeting the specific CPU of your build node, specify a more general architecture that's compatible with all cluster nodes. 2. **Check your cluster's CPU architecture** to determine the most portable target. On FASRC clusters, this is typically something like `x86_64` or a specific generation that's common across all nodes. 3. **Use Spack's `target` specification** in your build command. For example: ```bash spack install --target=x86_64 your_package ``` Or in your Spack environment/config, you can specify the target architecture. 4. **Avoid overly aggressive optimization flags** that target specific CPU features. You can control compiler flags through Spack's configuration. For detailed guidance on Spack configuration and architecture targeting, refer to the [SPACK Package Manager documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager/). Would you like help with specific Spack commands or configuration for your use case?
✅ Expected Answer
By default Spack autodetects the architecture of the hardware it is building on and optimizes the build for it, so a build made on one CPU generation may not run on another (the docs give the example that LAMMPS built on Sapphire Rapids may not run on Cascade Lake). If you plan to run across heterogeneous hardware/partitions, set Spack to a generic architecture: edit `etc/spack/packages.yaml` inside the spack folder (create it if it does not exist) and add: packages: all: target: [x86_64]
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: spack target architecture portable build across nodes

Question 79: question_79

❓ Question

I don't want spack spending hours compiling its own OpenMPI — how do I make it use the openmpi module we already have on the cluster, and is there anything I have to do before installing?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/FFTW/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Parallel_HDF5/Example2/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To make Spack use the cluster's existing `openmpi` module instead of compiling its own, you need to tell Spack to treat it as an **external package**. Here's how: ### 1. Load the module first Before running any Spack commands, load the OpenMPI module you want Spack to use: ```bash module load openmpi/<version>-fasrcXX ``` ### 2. Discover external packages Run `spack external find` to have Spack scan your environment for already-installed software (including modules): ```bash spack external find ``` This will detect the loaded OpenMPI module and add it to your `~/.spack/packages.yaml` file as an external package [Spack Package Manager – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). ### 3. Verify and optionally lock it Check that Spack sees the external OpenMPI: ```bash spack find openmpi ``` If you want to **prevent Spack from ever building its own OpenMPI**, add a `buildable: false` entry for it in your `~/.spack/packages.yaml`: ```yaml packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@<version> prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/MPI/gcc/<compiler>/openmpi/<version> buildable: false ``` You can find the correct `prefix` path by running `module show openmpi/<version>` or `which mpirun` after loading the module. ### Key point As the docs note, Spack will normally build its own package stack even if libraries are available on the system. You **must** have the module loaded *before* running `spack external find` for it to be detected [Spack Package Manager – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). Want help finding the exact prefix path for your OpenMPI version?
✅ Expected Answer
Configure the cluster's OpenMPI as an external package in `~/.spack/packages.yaml` (create the file if it does not exist). Get the prefix by loading the modules and reading $MPI_HOME, then add: packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@5.0.5%gcc@14.2.0 prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/Comp/gcc/14.2.0-fasrc01/openmpi/5.0.5-fasrc01 buildable: false The `buildable: false` option ensures MPI won't be built from source and Spack will use the module in that prefix instead. Before installing, run `module purge` — this is required or the build fails (e.g. `module purge` then `spack install hdf5@1.14.6 % gcc@14.2.0 ^ openmpi@5.0.5`).

🤖 Assistant Message #15
To make Spack use the cluster's existing `openmpi` module instead of compiling its own, you need to tell Spack to treat it as an **external package**. Here's how: ### 1. Load the module first Before running any Spack commands, load the OpenMPI module you want Spack to use: ```bash module load openmpi/<version>-fasrcXX ``` ### 2. Discover external packages Run `spack external find` to have Spack scan your environment for already-installed software (including modules): ```bash spack external find ``` This will detect the loaded OpenMPI module and add it to your `~/.spack/packages.yaml` file as an external package [Spack Package Manager – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). ### 3. Verify and optionally lock it Check that Spack sees the external OpenMPI: ```bash spack find openmpi ``` If you want to **prevent Spack from ever building its own OpenMPI**, add a `buildable: false` entry for it in your `~/.spack/packages.yaml`: ```yaml packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@<version> prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/MPI/gcc/<compiler>/openmpi/<version> buildable: false ``` You can find the correct `prefix` path by running `module show openmpi/<version>` or `which mpirun` after loading the module. ### Key point As the docs note, Spack will normally build its own package stack even if libraries are available on the system. You **must** have the module loaded *before* running `spack external find` for it to be detected [Spack Package Manager – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). Want help finding the exact prefix path for your OpenMPI version?

Question 80: question_80

❓ Question

We cleaned out several TB of old data last month but this month's storage charge came in exactly the same. Is the bill wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spinal-expense-codes, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The bill is likely correct. According to FASRC's [Data Storage Billing](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing/) documentation: > The monthly charge for RC Storage is based on the storage tier and the **total size of the allocation**, not the amount of used space. As a result, **deleting files will not reduce your monthly cost unless the allocation size is also reduced**. In other words, storage charges are based on how much storage you've been allocated (e.g., 10 TiB), not how much of it you're actually using. Deleting old data frees up space, but it doesn't change the size of your allocation — so the bill stays the same. If you want to reduce your storage costs, you'd need to **reduce the allocation size** itself. Keep in mind that billing changes are determined on the **15th of each month**, so any changes made after that date won't be reflected until the following month's bill. If you believe there's an error on your bill, you can contact [RC Storage Billing](mailto:billing@rc.fas.harvard.edu) within 3 business days of receiving the notification.
✅ Expected Answer
No, the bill is correct. The monthly charge is based on the size of the storage allocation, not on the amount of storage actually used, so deleting files will not reduce the charge unless the allocation size itself is also reduced. Separately, the cutoff for billing changes is the 15th of each month — any change made to an allocation after the 15th is only reflected on the following month's bill.

Question 82: question_82

❓ Question

Every time I try to map my Cannon home drive on my Windows laptop I end up locked out of my account. How many bad tries does that take, how long am I locked out for, and what am I doing wrong in the Map Network Drive box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unlock_account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here is what is happening: ### How many bad tries and how long is the lockout? According to the [Locked Account](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unlock_account/) guide, your account locks after entering the incorrect password **multiple times**. The lockout is not permanent and will **automatically unlock after about 5–10 minutes**. If it remains locked longer than that, you should contact FASRC support. ### What you are doing wrong The most common reason for repeated lockouts when mapping a network drive is an incorrect username format. When prompted for credentials in the **Map Network Drive** box on Windows, you must prepend your FASRC username with the **`RC\`** domain specifier. * **Incorrect:** `your_username` * **Correct:** `RC\your_username` ### Correct steps to map your Cannon home drive 1. **Connect to the VPN:** Cluster storage must be routed through the Research Computing VPN. 2. **Find your home directory path:** SSH into Cannon and run `cd ~` to see your home directory path (e.g., `/n/home13/your_username`). The folder name (e.g., `home13`) is the part you need. 3. **Map the drive:** * Open File Explorer and select **This PC**. * Click **Map network drive** and choose a drive letter. * In the **Folder** box, enter the path: `\\rcstore.rc.fas.harvard.edu\homes\homeXX\your_username` (replace `homeXX` with your actual home directory folder, like `home13`). * When prompted for credentials, enter **`RC\your_username`** as the username and your FASRC password. For more details, see the [Mounting Storage on Desktop or Laptop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage/) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
You are almost certainly letting Windows authenticate with your local PC account. In the Map Network Drive dialog you must tick "Connect using different credentials"; if you don't, Windows tries your local PC information and after three failed attempts you are locked out. The lockout expires automatically in about 5 minutes. When prompted for network credentials, prepend your FASRC username with RC\ (for example RC\jharvard) so you authenticate against the RC domain.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Question 83: question_83

❓ Question

I'm on a collaborator's network where I can't run a second VPN client. Can I still get files off the cluster with FileZilla, or is the VPN mandatory for everything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-client-update, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/license-server, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Yes, you can use FileZilla without the VPN** — the FASRC documentation explicitly states that if you are just SSH'ing (or using SFTP via FileZilla) to the login nodes, **VPN is not required** [VPN Setup](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup/). The VPN is mandatory only for specific services like: * Mounting shared drives to your laptop/desktop * Accessing Open OnDemand (VDI) * Accessing level 3 environments * License server access * Accessing Starfish For standard SFTP file transfers using FileZilla, you can connect directly to the login nodes: * **Cannon:** `login.rc.fas.harvard.edu` * **FASSE:** `fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu` Just make sure you use **SFTP – SSH File Transfer Protocol** with **Interactive** login type so you're prompted for your OpenAuth token [SFTP file transfer using Filezilla (Mac/Windows/Linux)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/sftp-file-transfer/).
✅ Expected Answer
You can still use SFTP. Mounting cluster storage as a drive does require the VPN — cluster storage must be routed through a VPN connection — but SFTP to a login node does not require a FASRC/FAS VPN connection, because login nodes use two-factor authentication. FASRC recommends FileZilla as a cross-platform SFTP client; you need OpenAuth set up plus cluster access and a home directory.

Question 84: question_84

❓ Question

My home dir is full and now rm won't even work — it says 'No space left on device' when I try to delete something. How am I supposed to clean up if I can't delete anything?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_nompi.cu, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a known edge case: when your home directory is over quota, the filesystem won't let you allocate new blocks, so even `rm` can fail with **"No space left on device"** because the deletion operation itself needs a tiny bit of free space to update directory metadata. ### Workaround: truncate files to zero bytes first Instead of deleting, shrink the largest files to 0 bytes using `truncate`: ```bash truncate -s 0 ~/path/to/bigfile.out ``` Or, if `truncate` also fails: ```bash cat /dev/null > ~/path/to/bigfile ``` Once you've freed enough space to get **under quota**, `rm` will work normally again. ### Find what's using space ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` If `df` says you're over quota but `du` shows less, you may have **sparse files**. In that case, use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` ### Watch out for `~/.local` The `~/.local` folder often balloons from `pip install` outside virtual environments. If it's the culprit, stop any running jobs (`squeue -u <username>`), then rename it: ```bash mv ~/.local ~/.local.bak ``` This frees space immediately; you can recreate a clean `.local` later. Let me know if you'd like help identifying the biggest files or directories to target.
✅ Expected Answer
Shrink a file to zero bytes instead of unlinking it. Pick a large file you intend to remove and run `truncate -s 0 FILENAME` (you can list several files: `truncate -s 0 FILE1 FILE2 FILE3`), or equivalently redirect the empty device into it with `cat /dev/null > ~/mybig.file`. Once enough space is recovered to get you back under quota, `rm` will work again. Use full paths when deleting to avoid removing the wrong file.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Args: No query found.

Question 85: question_85

❓ Question

Quota says I'm at my limit but when I add up my folders with du it's way less than that. Which one is lying, and how do I find whatever is eating the space?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-billing

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Neither is necessarily lying** — the discrepancy is usually caused by one of two things: ### 1. Deleted files still held open by running processes When a file is deleted (`rm`) but a process still has it open, the space isn't freed until the process closes it or restarts. You can check for this with: ```bash lsof +L1 ``` This lists files that have been deleted but are still held open. Restarting the relevant job/process will free the space. ### 2. Sparse files Sparse files are files that appear large in `du`'s default output but don't actually consume that much disk space. The filesystem quota tracks the *actual* allocated blocks, not the apparent size. To see the true picture, use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` This shows the **logical/apparent size** rather than the disk blocks used, which should align more closely with what `quota` reports. ### How to find what's eating space Once you know which directory is the culprit: ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` Then drill down into the largest directories to find specific files. For hidden folders like `~/.local` (which can grow from `pip install` outside of virtual environments), check those as well. ### If you're over quota and can't delete files If `rm` gives "No space left on device," you can truncate files to zero bytes to free space: ```bash truncate -s 0 FILENAME ``` Or: ```bash cat /dev/null > FILENAME ``` Once you're under quota, you can then use `rm` normally.
✅ Expected Answer
Neither is lying — you likely have sparse files, whose space is counted by the filesystem's quota check (what `df` reports) but not accounted for properly by `du`. To find them, show the logical size instead: `cd ~` then `du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 .` That points you at the directories responsible.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full
💬 Agent Messages (5)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: Quota says I'm at my limit but when I add up my folders with du it's way less than that. Which one is lying, and how do I find whatever is eating the space?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🛠️ Tool Call #3: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: du vs quota discrepancy deleted files space not freed
🛠️ Tool Call #4: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #5
**Neither is necessarily lying** — the discrepancy is usually caused by one of two things: ### 1. Deleted files still held open by running processes When a file is deleted (`rm`) but a process still has it open, the space isn't freed until the process closes it or restarts. You can check for this with: ```bash lsof +L1 ``` This lists files that have been deleted but are still held open. Restarting the relevant job/process will free the space. ### 2. Sparse files Sparse files are files that appear large in `du`'s default output but don't actually consume that much disk space. The filesystem quota tracks the *actual* allocated blocks, not the apparent size. To see the true picture, use: ```bash du -ch --apparent-size --max-depth=1 . ``` This shows the **logical/apparent size** rather than the disk blocks used, which should align more closely with what `quota` reports. ### How to find what's eating space Once you know which directory is the culprit: ```bash cd ~ du -h --max-depth=1 . ``` Then drill down into the largest directories to find specific files. For hidden folders like `~/.local` (which can grow from `pip install` outside of virtual environments), check those as well. ### If you're over quota and can't delete files If `rm` gives "No space left on device," you can truncate files to zero bytes to free space: ```bash truncate -s 0 FILENAME ``` Or: ```bash cat /dev/null > FILENAME ``` Once you're under quota, you can then use `rm` normally.

Question 86: question_86

❓ Question

openconnect on my Ubuntu box dies right after it authenticates — something about not being able to find a script to configure the tunnel. How do I fix that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vpn-setup, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The error you're seeing is because OpenConnect can't find the **vpnc-script** that configures the tunnel after authentication. Here's how to fix it: 1. **Install the vpnc-script package** (if you don't already have it): ```bash sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts ``` 2. **Find the script location** and make sure it's executable. For example: ```bash sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script ``` 3. **If OpenConnect still can't find it**, specify the script path explicitly when connecting: ```bash openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/ ``` (Adjust the path to match where the script actually lives on your system.) This is documented in the FASRC guide for connecting to the VPN from Linux [Connecting to the FAS RC VPN from Linux clients](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/linux-vpn/).
✅ Expected Answer
OpenConnect needs vpnc-script. Install it if you don't have it (Ubuntu/Debian: `sudo apt-get install vpnc-scripts`; Fedora/CentOS: `sudo dnf install vpnc-script`; Arch: `sudo pacman -S vpnc`), make sure it is executable (e.g. `sudo chmod u+x /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script`), and if OpenConnect still can't find it, pass its location explicitly: `openconnect --script /etc/vpnc/vpnc-script https://vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu/` (substituting the actual path on your system).

Question 88: question_88

❓ Question

I hit Ctrl-C on my srun job to stop it and it just printed some status about the tasks and kept running. How do I actually make it stop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a known behavior with `srun`. When you press `Ctrl-C`, `srun` prints a status message but **does not automatically kill the tasks** it launched. The tasks continue running in the background. To actually stop the job, you need to explicitly send a termination signal to the job step. Here are your options: 1. **Use `scancel` on the job step ID**: If you know the job step ID (e.g., `12345.0`), run: ```bash scancel 12345.0 ``` If you're inside an interactive `srun` session, you can often use `scancel` with the job ID of the allocation. 2. **Send a signal to the srun process itself**: In some configurations, sending a stronger signal (like `SIGKILL`) to the `srun` process will also terminate the tasks. You can find the PID of the `srun` process and kill it: ```bash kill -9 <srun_pid> ``` 3. **Use `--kill-on-bad-exit` or `--signal` options**: When launching `srun`, you can specify how it handles signals. For example, using `--signal=B:TERM` can help ensure tasks are terminated when `srun` receives a signal. The key takeaway is that `Ctrl-C` on `srun` is **not** a reliable way to stop running tasks. You need to explicitly cancel the job step using `scancel` or kill the tasks directly. For more details, see the [Slurm FAQ on srun termination behavior](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html).
✅ Expected Answer
That is by design: a single <control-c> is an escape sequence that reports the state of all tasks associated with the srun command. To kill the job, press <control-c> twice within one second — that sends SIGINT to all tasks and starts a termination sequence of SIGCONT, SIGTERM and SIGKILL to all spawned tasks. A third <control-c> terminates srun itself immediately, without waiting for the remote tasks to exit or their I/O to complete. (Signal forwarding does not work at all for asynchronous steps launched with --async; use scancel --signal for those.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Question 89: question_89

❓ Question

I asked for several tasks in an interactive srun --pty session, but only one of them shows any output and the rest die instantly. What is --pty doing to the others?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/nss_slurm.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, slurm.schedmd.com/MISC25/Slinky-KubeConEurope2025.pdf, github.com/OSC/ood-documentation/blob/release-4.1/source/conf.py

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The `--pty` flag tells `srun` to allocate a **pseudo-terminal (PTY)** for the job step. A PTY is a single terminal device that provides line editing, signal handling, and interactive I/O. By design, **only one task (rank 0) can be attached to a PTY** — the others have no terminal to read from or write to. Here's what's happening: - **Task 0** gets the PTY, so its stdout/stderr is visible on your terminal and it can read interactive input. - **Tasks 1+** have no PTY attached. If they try to write to stdout/stderr, that output goes nowhere (or to a null device), and if they try to read from stdin, they get EOF or hang. In many cases, this causes them to exit immediately or appear to "die." This is why you see output from only one task while the others vanish. ### What to do instead - **For multi-task interactive debugging**, use `srun` without `--pty` and redirect output to files, or use `salloc` + `srun` with explicit I/O redirection. - **If you need all tasks to be interactive**, you'll need separate terminal sessions (e.g., SSH into each node or use a tool like `tmux`/`screen` on each node) rather than a single `--pty` step. - **For batch multi-task jobs**, use `sbatch` and let Slurm capture stdout/stderr to log files automatically. For more detail, see the Slurm FAQ on the difference between `sbatch` and `srun` [Slurm Workload Manager - Frequently Asked Questions](https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html) and the `srun` documentation [Slurm Workload Manager - srun](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).
✅ Expected Answer
--pty runs only task zero under a pseudo terminal. It implicitly sets --error and --output to /dev/null for every task except task zero, which can make those other tasks exit immediately — shells in particular typically exit right away in that situation. --pty also implicitly sets --unbuffered, and it applies to step allocations.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

Question 90: question_90

❓ Question

I added two more #SBATCH lines further down my script, below my module load commands, and Slurm completely ignored them. I also tried #SBATCH --job-name=$MYNAME and it came out literally as $MYNAME. Why?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/heterogeneous_jobs.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Two separate issues are at play here: ### 1. `#SBATCH` lines ignored when placed later in the script Slurm only parses `#SBATCH` directives that appear **at the top of the batch script**, immediately after the shebang line (`#!/bin/bash` or similar). According to the FASRC SLURM examples, `#SBATCH` settings **must come before any other comments or code**, or they may not work [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md). Once Slurm encounters non-`#SBATCH` content (including regular comments, module loads, or any other lines), it stops parsing directives. That's why your two lines placed below `module load` commands were silently ignored. **Fix:** Move all `#SBATCH` lines to the very top of your script, right after the shebang, before any other lines. ### 2. `#SBATCH --job-name=$MYNAME` appearing literally as `$MYNAME` This happens because `sbatch` parses `#SBATCH` directives **before** the script is executed by the shell. At parse time, the shell variable `$MYNAME` has not been expanded yet, so Slurm sees the literal string `$MYNAME` and uses it as-is. **Fixes:** - **Set the job name directly:** Use `#SBATCH --job-name=my_job_name` with a literal string. - **Use `--export` or `--get-user-env`:** If you need environment variables, ensure they're exported in the environment before submission, or use Slurm's `--export` flag. - **Alternative approach:** If you truly need a dynamic name based on a variable, you can construct the `sbatch` command itself with the variable expanded, e.g.: ```bash sbatch --job-name="$MYNAME" my_script.sh ``` This passes the expanded value to `sbatch` on the command line rather than relying on the `#SBATCH` directive in the script.
✅ Expected Answer
Both behaviors are documented sbatch parsing rules. Slurm stops reading #SBATCH directives once the first non-comment, non-whitespace line in the script has been reached — so any #SBATCH placed after your module load (or any other command) is never processed. And #SBATCH directives are read directly by Slurm, not by the shell, so shell-specific syntax including variable names is read as literal text, which is why $MYNAME was not expanded. Put all #SBATCH lines at the top of the script, before any commands.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

Question 91: question_91

❓ Question

I ran scontrol hold on a job that was already running because I wanted to pause it, and it just kept going. What did that command actually do to my job, and can I pause a running job at all?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**What `scontrol hold` actually did:** It did **not** pause or suspend your running job. Instead, it set the job's priority to 0 and updated the job's reason field. As the Slurm documentation explains, "attempting to hold a running job will not suspend or cancel it. But, it will set the job priority to 0 and update the job reason field, which would hold the job if it was requeued at a later time" [Slurm Workload Manager - scontrol](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html). In other words, the hold only takes effect if the job later finishes and gets requeued — it won't stop a job that's already running. **Can you pause a running job?** Slurm does have a suspend/resume mechanism, but it's not typically used by end users for ad-hoc pausing. The Gang Scheduling documentation notes that "manually suspending a job (i.e. 'scontrol suspend ...') releases its CPUs for allocation to other jobs" [Slurm Workload Manager - Gang Scheduling](https://slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html). However, this is generally an administrative operation and may have implications for gang scheduling and CPU allocation. If you need to temporarily stop a job's execution, you might consider: - Sending `SIGSTOP` to the job's processes (e.g., `kill -STOP <pid>`) and later `SIGCONT` to resume them — though the Gang Scheduling docs warn this "would stop and start the job's tasks without releasing the CPUs" [Slurm Workload Manager - Gang Scheduling](https://slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html) - Designing your script to check for a "pause" flag file and sleep/wait when needed For most use cases, if you need to stop a job entirely, `scancel` is the standard approach. Let me know if you'd like help with any of these alternatives.
✅ Expected Answer
Holding a running job does not suspend or cancel it. What it does is set the job's priority to 0 and update the job's reason field, which would hold the job if it were requeued at a later time. Actually pausing a running job is the `suspend` command, and only a privileged user or an account coordinator can suspend jobs (time spent suspended does not count against the job's time limit, and a suspended job that is requeued is placed in a held state). Note also that a hold placed by an ordinary user is a user-hold, while one placed by a privileged user is an admin-hold, and the hold type determines who can `release` it.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I ran scontrol hold on a job that was already running because I wanted to pause it, and it just kept going. What did that command actually do to my job, and can I pause a running job at all?

The Gang Scheduling documentation notes that "manually suspending a job (i.e. 'scontrol suspend ...') releases its CPUs for allocation to other jobs" [Slurm Workload Manager - Gang Scheduling](https://slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html). However, this is generally an administrative operation and may have implications for gang scheduling and CPU allocation. If you need to temporarily stop a job's execution, you might consider: - Sending `SIGSTOP` to the job's processes (e.g., `kill -STOP <pid>`) and later `SIGCONT` to resume them — though the Gang Scheduling docs warn this "would stop and start the job's tasks without releasing the CPUs" [Slurm Workload Manager - Gang Scheduling](https://slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html) - Designing your script to check for a "pause" flag file and sleep/wait when needed For most use cases, if you need to stop a job entirely, `scancel` is the standard approach. Let me know if you'd like help with any of these alternatives.

Question 92: question_92

❓ Question

I overwrote my submit script by accident but the job is still sitting in the queue. Is there any way to get the original script back out of Slurm?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Fortran/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, **Slurm does not store the original submit script** in a retrievable location once the job is submitted. When you submit a job with `sbatch`, Slurm reads the script and executes it, but it does **not** keep a copy of the script file itself on the filesystem or in its database. ### What you can try: 1. **Check for backups or version control** If you use Git, check your repository history: ```bash git log --oneline -- your_script.sh git show HEAD:your_script.sh ``` If you use `rsync`, `tar`, or other backup tools, check those. 2. **Check for temporary copies** Sometimes editors create backup files (e.g., `script.sh.bak`, `script.sh~`). Look for those: ```bash ls -la script.sh* ``` 3. **Check your job's stdout/stderr** If your script printed any of its own content (e.g., via `cat $0`), that output may be in the job's log files: ```bash cat slurm-<jobid>.out ``` 4. **Reconstruct the script** If you have no backups, you'll need to recreate it from memory or notes. ### For the future: - Keep submit scripts in version control (Git). - Use a backup strategy (e.g., `rsync` to a backup directory). - Consider using `sbatch --export=ALL` with environment files if you need to separate configuration from the script. If you'd like, I can help you reconstruct the script if you remember its key components (partition, resources, commands, etc.).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — use `scontrol write batch_script <job_id> [optional_filename]`. It writes the batch script that was submitted for that job to a file, defaulting to slurm-<job_id>.sh if you don't give a filename; pass `-` instead of a filename to write it to stdout. Only a privileged user or the owner of the job can retrieve the script.

Question 93: question_93

❓ Question

I want to grab a couple of nodes and sit on them for a while, but I don't want salloc to drop me into a shell that I have to keep open. Can I reserve the resources and then send work at them later from my normal terminal?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/parallel-matlab-pct-dcs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, you can reserve resources with `salloc` without being dropped into an interactive shell. The key is to specify a command (like `sleep infinity` or `tail -f /dev/null`) instead of letting `salloc` launch a shell: ```bash salloc -N 2 -t 02:00:00 sleep infinity ``` This allocates 2 nodes for 2 hours and keeps the allocation alive without giving you an interactive shell. You can then submit jobs to those nodes later using `srun` or `sbatch` from your normal terminal, as long as the allocation is still active. Alternatively, you can use `salloc` with `--no-shell` if your Slurm version supports it: ```bash salloc --no-shell -N 2 -t 02:00:00 ``` This reserves the resources without spawning any shell at all. You can then use `srun` to launch commands on the allocated nodes: ```bash srun -N 2 -n 4 my_command ``` The allocation will remain active until the walltime expires or you cancel it with `scancel <job_id>`. For more details, see the [Slurm Workload Manager - salloc](https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Use `salloc --no-shell`. It exits immediately after allocating the resources without running a command, but the Slurm job is still created and stays active, owning the allocated resources for as long as it lives — you get a Slurm job id with no associated processes or tasks. You can then run work against it by passing `--jobid=<that job id>` to srun. The job is still subject to normal constraints such as time limits, so it will eventually end on its own, or you can end it early with scancel.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html

Question 95: question_95

❓ Question

Our compute nodes have hyperthreading turned on (2 threads per core). If my job asks for 3 tasks, does Slurm hand me exactly 3 hyperthreads, and could another user's job end up sharing a core with mine?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the Slurm documentation, here's what happens with hyperthreading: **Slurm does NOT guarantee that your 3 tasks will occupy only 3 hyperthreads.** The behavior depends on the `--threads-per-core` setting (or `--hint`): - **If `--threads-per-core` is NOT specified** (the default in many configurations), Slurm may pack multiple tasks onto the same physical core. For example, two tasks can run on separate hyperthreads of the same core, and a third task may use both threads of a second core. In this case, **another user's job could absolutely share a physical core with yours**, since Slurm doesn't reserve the full core exclusively. - **If you request `--threads-per-core=1`** (or use `--hint=nomultithread`), Slurm will reserve only one hyperthread per task and leave the other hyperthread on that core unused by your job. This prevents other jobs from using that second hyperthread, but it also means you're not utilizing the full hardware. - **If you request `--threads-per-core=2`** (or don't restrict it), Slurm may allocate both hyperthreads of a core to your job even if you only requested one task. The memory-per-CPU calculation will include both threads. **Key takeaway:** If you want to ensure another user's job does NOT share a physical core with yours, you need to explicitly request `--hint=exclusive` or `--hint=nomultithread` in your job script. Otherwise, Slurm may pack tasks onto shared cores, and other jobs can use the remaining hyperthreads on those cores. For more details, see the Slurm srun documentation on [hyperthreading and threads-per-core](https://slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html).
✅ Expected Answer
With hyperthreading enabled a "CPU" in Slurm means a hyperthread (without it, a CPU is a core), but Slurm still generally allocates resources to jobs at the level of a whole core. A job requesting three tasks on a node with ThreadsPerCore=2 is therefore allocated two full cores (four threads), not three threads. Two different jobs will not share a core unless the partition's OverSubscribe configuration parameter allows it. You can check ThreadsPerCore with "scontrol show node". The exception is the atypical configuration where the admin sets SelectTypeParameters=CR_CPU with only a CPU count per node (no socket/core/thread spec) — then each thread is scheduled independently as a CPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Question 96: question_96

❓ Question

My MPI run blows up about half a minute after one rank finishes early — the rest of the ranks were still working. Can I stop Slurm from tearing the whole thing down that fast?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclAllGather_mpi.cu, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/ncclReduceScatter_mpi.cu, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/MPI_and_CUDA/mpi_cuda_2.cu, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_exit_code.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to diagnose why Slurm terminates an MPI job prematurely when one rank exits early by searching the vector store for relevant Slurm configuration and MPI behavior documentation. However, the search results primarily returned generic code examples for MPI+CUDA distributed training (e.g., NCCL, PyTorch DDP) rather than specific Slurm job management settings. **Answer:** Yes, you can prevent Slurm from killing the remaining ranks when one MPI process exits early. This behavior is controlled by the Slurm configuration parameter `KillOnBadExit`. 1. **Immediate Fix (Job Script):** Add the following directive to your Slurm job script: ```bash #SBATCH --kill-on-bad-exit=0 ``` Setting this to `0` (or `NO`) tells Slurm not to send a signal to the job step if any process in the job exits with a non-zero status. This allows the other ranks to continue running and potentially finish their work or handle the failure gracefully. 2. **System-Wide Fix:** If you do not have permission to modify job scripts, contact your system administrator to change the `KillOnBadExit` setting in the Slurm `slurm.conf` file to `NO`. 3. **Application-Level Fix:** Ensure your MPI application handles the early exit gracefully. Often, the "blow up" is due to the remaining ranks trying to communicate with the finished rank and encountering a broken pipe or connection reset. Using `MPI_Comm_set_errhandler` or ensuring all ranks call `MPI_Finalize` (even if one exited early, though this is tricky) can help. However, `--kill-on-bad-exit=0` is the most direct Slurm-level solution to keep the job alive. **Note:** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50, so further specific configuration searches were not completed.
✅ Expected Answer
That is srun's default behavior, not a crash: srun monitors when tasks exit and by default kills the job 30 seconds after the first task exits (producing "srun: First task exited 30s ago" followed by "srun Job Failed"). Use srun's --wait=<time> option to either lengthen the timeout period or disable the timeout altogether.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

Question 97: question_97

❓ Question

Why does sacct come back empty for a job I ran last Tuesday, but show it just fine as soon as I pass the job ID with -j?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, slurm.schedmd.com/federation.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise3/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
This is a common and frustrating behavior with `sacct`. Here's what's happening: ## Why `sacct` appears empty without `-j` By default, `sacct` only shows jobs that are **currently running** or **recently completed**. SLURM has a configuration parameter called **`PastJobsLimit`** (or `SacctCount`/`SacctPurge`) that controls how many completed jobs are retained in the accounting database before they are purged. If your job from last Tuesday has fallen outside this retention window, it won't show up in a general `sacct` query. ## Why `-j <jobid>` works When you pass a specific job ID with `-j <jobid>`, `sacct` queries the accounting database directly for that job, bypassing the default filtering. Even if the job is outside the normal retention window, the direct lookup can still retrieve it if the record hasn't been fully purged from disk. ## How to fix/verify 1. **Check your SLURM configuration** (if you have access): Look for `PastJobsLimit` or `SacctCount` in `/etc/slurm/slurm.conf` or the FASRC equivalent. 2. **Use a date filter explicitly** to broaden your search: ```bash sacct --starttime 2024-01-09 --endtime 2024-01-15 ``` (Adjust dates to cover last Tuesday.) 3. **Use `sacct -u <username>` with a date range** to see all your jobs in that window: ```bash sacct -u <username> -S <YYYY-MM-DD> -E <YYYY-MM-DD> --format=JobID,JobName,State,Elapsed ``` See the [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/) page for more examples. 4. **If the job is truly purged**, the only way to retrieve it is if you saved the job ID and can use `sacct -j <jobid>` directly — but even that may fail if the record has been completely removed from disk. If you're consistently losing job history, consider: - Saving job IDs when you submit them - Using `seff <jobid>` or `jobstats <jobid>` immediately after completion to capture statistics - Checking with FASRC support about the retention policy on your cluster
✅ Expected Answer
sacct's default search window changes depending on which options you use. With neither --jobs nor --state specified, --starttime defaults to Midnight and --endtime defaults to Now, so a job from last week falls outside the window and nothing is returned. As soon as you specify --jobs, --starttime defaults to Epoch 0 (with --endtime still Now), so the old job is found. To list older jobs without a job ID, pass an explicit -S/--starttime. Running sacct with -v/--verbose prints the time window actually in use.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html
💬 Agent Messages (10)

Args: Why does sacct come back empty for a job I ran last Tuesday, but show it just fine as soon as I pass the job ID with -j?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Args: sacct empty no jobs found
🛠️ Tool Call #4: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: sacct date filter PastJobsLimit job state retention completed jobs
🛠️ Tool Call #5: search_local_files
Args: sacct PastJobsLimit
🛠️ Tool Call #6: search_local_files
Args: sacct empty no jobs
🛠️ Tool Call #7: search_local_files
Args: sacct -j job ID works but no filter returns nothing
🛠️ Tool Call #8: search_local_files
Args: sacct
🛠️ Tool Call #9: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #10
This is a common and frustrating behavior with `sacct`. Here's what's happening: ## Why `sacct` appears empty without `-j` By default, `sacct` only shows jobs that are **currently running** or **recently completed**. SLURM has a configuration parameter called **`PastJobsLimit`** (or `SacctCount`/`SacctPurge`) that controls how many completed jobs are retained in the accounting database before they are purged. If your job from last Tuesday has fallen outside this retention window, it won't show up in a general `sacct` query. ## Why `-j <jobid>` works When you pass a specific job ID with `-j <jobid>`, `sacct` queries the accounting database directly for that job, bypassing the default filtering. Even if the job is outside the normal retention window, the direct lookup can still retrieve it if the record hasn't been fully purged from disk. ## How to fix/verify 1. **Check your SLURM configuration** (if you have access): Look for `PastJobsLimit` or `SacctCount` in `/etc/slurm/slurm.conf` or the FASRC equivalent. 2. **Use a date filter explicitly** to broaden your search: ```bash sacct --starttime 2024-01-09 --endtime 2024-01-15 ``` (Adjust dates to cover last Tuesday.) 3. **Use `sacct -u <username>` with a date range** to see all your jobs in that window: ```bash sacct -u <username> -S <YYYY-MM-DD> -E <YYYY-MM-DD> --format=JobID,JobName,State,Elapsed ``` See the [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/) page for more examples. 4. **If the job is truly purged**, the only way to retrieve it is if you saved the job ID and can use `sacct -j <jobid>` directly — but even that may fail if the record has been completely removed from disk. If you're consistently losing job history, consider: - Saving job IDs when you submit them - Using `seff <jobid>` or `jobstats <jobid>` immediately after completion to capture statistics - Checking with FASRC support about the retention policy on your cluster

Question 98: question_98

❓ Question

sacct -X shows my completed job but every memory and CPU utilization column reads 0. Is our accounting broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Fortran/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/R/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise3/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to diagnose why `sacct -X` reports zero memory and CPU utilization for completed jobs by searching documentation and local files. The retrieved evidence confirms that Slurm does track these metrics (as shown in `seff` examples), but the `sacct` command requires specific format options to display them. **Your accounting is likely not broken.** The issue is almost certainly due to how the `sacct` command is formatted. By default, `sacct` may not display detailed resource utilization columns, or they may be hidden depending on the `DefaultAccountingFields` configuration in `slurm.conf`. To see the actual memory and CPU usage, you need to explicitly request those fields. Try running: ```bash sacct -X -o jobid,jobname,state,elapsed,maxrss,ncpus,cpu ``` Or, for a more user-friendly summary that includes efficiency metrics (which is often easier to read than raw `sacct` output), use the `seff` command: ```bash seff <job_id> ``` If `seff` also shows zeros or errors, then there may be a configuration issue with `SlurmctldParameters=accounting_storage_enforce` or `JobAcctGatherType` in your Slurm configuration, but in most cases, it is simply a matter of requesting the correct columns in `sacct`.
✅ Expected Answer
Not necessarily. -X/--allocations only shows statistics relevant to the job allocation itself and does not take job steps into consideration, and without including steps the utilization statistics for the allocation are reported as zero. Drop -X (or query the steps) to see real usage. Separately, which metrics exist at all depends on the jobacct_gather plugin in use — for example jobacct_gather/cgroup with cgroup/v2 cannot provide virtual-memory metrics and shows 0 for those fields.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html

Question 99: question_99

❓ Question

Is there a way to keep an eye on just my own jobs in the queue, refreshing every 30 seconds, without typing my username or wrapping the command in a shell loop?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrontab.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/high_throughput.html, slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sched_config.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—use the `watch` utility with `squeue` and the `--user` flag (which defaults to your own account): ```bash watch -n 30 squeue --user=$USER ``` - **`watch -n 30`** refreshes the output every 30 seconds. - **`squeue --user=$USER`** shows only jobs owned by you (no need to type your username manually). If you want a cleaner, less frequent update, you can also use: ```bash watch -n 30 -t squeue -u $USER ``` - **`-t`** suppresses the header showing the time of each refresh. - **`-u $USER`** is equivalent to `--user=$USER`. This avoids wrapping the command in a shell loop and doesn’t require hardcoding your username.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — run `squeue --me -i 30`. The --me option is equivalent to --user=<my username>, and -i/--iterate=<seconds> makes squeue repeatedly gather and report the requested information at the interval specified (printing a time stamp with the header by default). Note the man page's PERFORMANCE warning: executing squeue sends a remote procedure call to slurmctld, so do not run squeue from loops in shell scripts or other programs, and keep calls to the minimum necessary — enough of them can degrade slurmctld and even cause a denial of service.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html

Question 100: question_100

❓ Question

I want a one-line-per-partition view of how many nodes are busy versus free, but sinfo keeps giving me several rows per partition. What do I run, and how do I read the counts it prints?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/pdfs/summary.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres_share.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Run: ```bash sinfo --summarize ``` This prints **one line per partition** instead of one line per node/state combination. The output columns are: | Column | Meaning | |--------|---------| | **PARTITION** | Partition name | | **avail\_to\_start** | Nodes that are idle and available to start jobs | | **nodes** | Total nodes in the partition | | **state\_summary** | A compact summary of node states (e.g. `idle+down`, `alloc+idle`) | | **nodes\_per\_state** | Breakdown of how many nodes are in each state | ### How to read it - **`avail_to_start`** = nodes that are **idle** (free and ready). - **`nodes`** = total nodes in that partition. - **`state_summary`** shows the distinct states present, e.g. `alloc+idle` means some nodes are allocated (busy) and some are idle (free). - **`nodes_per_state`** gives the exact count per state, e.g. `alloc:5 idle:10` means **5 busy**, **10 free**. So for a quick "busy vs. free" snapshot, look at: - **`avail_to_start`** → free nodes - **`nodes` − `avail_to_start`** → busy nodes (allocated, draining, etc.) Or parse the `nodes_per_state` column for a precise breakdown. See the `sinfo` man page for full format details [Slurm Workload Manager - sinfo](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Run `sinfo -s` (--summarize), which lists only a partition state summary with no node state details (internally it uses the format "%#P %.5a %.10l %.16F %N"). In that output the NODES(A/I/O/T) column is the count of nodes by state in the form allocated/idle/other/total — so, for example, 2/6/0/8 means 2 allocated, 6 idle, 0 other, 8 total.

  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Question 101: question_101

❓ Question

Several nodes are offline and I want to see the note the admins left about why. Which sinfo option shows those notes, and how much of each note do I actually get to see?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spinal-usage-guide-for-researchers, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/strigger.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/MISC25/Slinky-KubeConEurope2025.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, slurm.schedmd.com/rosetta.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/MISC25/Slinky-CUG2025.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/network.html, slurm.schedmd.com/overview.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the Slurm documentation, the option you want is **`-R`** (or **`--reason`**). ### How to use it Run: ```bash sinfo -R ``` This will list nodes that are down, drained, or draining along with the **reason** administrators have attached to each node's state. ### How much of the note do you see? The **`-R`** flag displays the full reason string that admins have set for each node. In the examples from the [Slurm sinfo documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html), you can see full reason messages like: - `Memory errors` - `Not Responding` The reason field is printed in its entirety for each affected node, so you'll see the complete admin note as long as it fits within the terminal width. If a reason is very long, it may wrap to the next line, but no truncation is applied by the flag itself.
✅ Expected Answer
Use `sinfo -R` (--list-reasons). It lists the reasons nodes are in the down, drained, fail or failing state — the administrator-supplied "reason" string — but it displays only the first 20 characters of the reason field, along with the list of nodes carrying that reason. Adding -l (--long) also includes the current node state in the output.

Question 102: question_102

❓ Question

I compiled my binary in my home directory on the login node and the job can't find it once it lands on a compute node. Does the scheduler copy my files out to the nodes for me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/hdf5_profile_user_guide.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the scheduler does **not** copy your files to the compute nodes. Your home directory (`$HOME`) is a network-mounted filesystem (typically NFS) that is accessible from both login and compute nodes. When your job runs on a compute node, it should be able to access files in your home directory directly over the network—**provided the binary was compiled correctly and is in a location the compute node can reach**. However, there are a few common reasons your job might "not find" the binary: 1. **PATH issues**: The compute node may not have the same `$PATH` as your login shell. Use the **full path** to your binary in your job script (e.g., `/home/username/mydir/mybinary` instead of just `mybinary`). 2. **Compilation on login nodes**: Compiling on a login node can sometimes lead to issues if the compiler environment differs from what's available on compute nodes. It's best practice to compile on a compute node or use a module environment that matches the compute nodes. 3. **Missing shared libraries**: If your binary depends on shared libraries, those libraries must also be accessible from the compute node. Use `ldd mybinary` to check for missing libraries. 4. **Scratch vs. home**: For performance, it's recommended to copy your binary and input files to scratch (`/n/netscratch`) before running, and copy results back afterward. Scratch is much faster for I/O-heavy jobs. **Quick fix**: Try running your job with the full absolute path to the binary, and make sure any required libraries are available on the compute nodes. For more on best practices, see the documentation on [Scratch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch/) and [submitting jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. Slurm does not automatically migrate executable or data files to the nodes allocated to a job — the files must already exist on local disk on those nodes or on a global file system such as NFS or Lustre. If you do want a copy on the compute nodes' local storage, use sbcast, which transfers a file to local disk on the allocated nodes using Slurm's hierarchical communications (e.g. `sbcast a.out /tmp/joe.a.out`, then run it with srun and delete it from local storage afterwards).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html
