Question 103: question_103

❓ Question

I ran scancel -s KILL on my job. The processes died, but the job is still sitting there holding its nodes. Why didn't it go away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/burst_buffer.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/gang_scheduling.html, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you run `scancel -s KILL`, Slurm sends the signal to the job's processes, but the job state doesn't always transition to `CANCELLED` immediately. Here's what's likely happening: ### Common reasons the job appears "stuck" 1. **Job is in `COMPLETING` state** — After receiving the signal, Slurm often moves the job to `COMPLETING` while it waits for all tasks to exit and for cleanup (like stage-out) to finish. This state can persist for a while if the job has a lot of output to flush or if cleanup scripts are running. 2. **Signal was sent but processes didn't die** — `KILL` (SIGKILL) should terminate processes immediately, but if the processes are in an uninterruptible sleep (e.g., stuck on I/O), they may not respond to signals. In that case, the job won't complete until the I/O resolves. 3. **Slurmctld hasn't processed the cancellation yet** — The signal is sent via RPC to `slurmctld`, which then propagates it. If there's a delay in the controller or if the job has many steps, the state transition can lag. ### What to do - **Check the job state** with `squeue -j <jobid>` or `scontrol show job <jobid>` to see if it's in `COMPLETING`, `CANCELLED`, or another state. - **If it's in `COMPLETING`**, wait a bit — Slurm will eventually release the nodes once all tasks exit and cleanup finishes. - **If it's stuck in `COMPLETING` for a long time**, contact FASRC support. They can investigate whether the job is truly hung or if there's a Slurm controller issue. - **For future reference**, you can use `scancel --hurry` to skip stage-out and force a faster teardown, though this may result in lost output. Would you like help checking the current state of your job?
✅ Expected Answer
Because sending a signal value of KILL to an entire job cancels the job's active job steps but does not cancel the job itself, and cancelling a job step never terminates the job — the job must be cancelled to release its resource allocation. To actually cancel it, invoke scancel with no --signal option: that sends a SIGCONT to all steps to wake them up, then a SIGTERM, waits the KillWait duration defined in slurm.conf, and finally sends SIGKILL if they have not terminated.

  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

When you run `scancel -s KILL`, Slurm sends the signal to the job's processes, but the job state doesn't always transition to `CANCELLED` immediately. Here's what's likely happening: ### Common reasons the job appears "stuck" 1. **Job is in `COMPLETING` state** — After receiving the signal, Slurm often moves the job to `COMPLETING` while it waits for all tasks to exit and for cleanup (like stage-out) to finish. This state can persist for a while if the job has a lot of output to flush or if cleanup scripts are running. 2. **Signal was sent but processes didn't die** — `KILL` (SIGKILL) should terminate processes immediately, but if the processes are in an uninterruptible sleep (e.g., stuck on I/O), they may not respond to signals. In that case, the job won't complete until the I/O resolves. 3. **Slurmctld hasn't processed the cancellation yet** — The signal is sent via RPC to `slurmctld`, which then propagates it. If there's a delay in the controller or if the job has many steps, the state transition can lag. ### What to do - **Check the job state** with `squeue -j <jobid>` or `scontrol show job <jobid>` to see if it's in `COMPLETING`, `CANCELLED`, or another state. - **If it's in `COMPLETING`**, wait a bit — Slurm will eventually release the nodes once all tasks exit and cleanup finishes. - **If it's stuck in `COMPLETING` for a long time**, contact FASRC support. They can investigate whether the job is truly hung or if there's a Slurm controller issue. - **For future reference**, you can use `scancel --hurry` to skip stage-out and force a faster teardown, though this may result in lost output. Question 104: question_104

❓ Question

Can I wipe out all of my queued-but-not-yet-started jobs in one partition with a single command, without touching the ones already running and without listing job IDs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sched_config.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—you can do this with a single `scancel` command that filters by **user**, **partition**, and **state**, without listing individual job IDs: ```bash scancel --user=$USER --partition=<PARTITION_NAME> --state=PENDING ``` ### How it works - **`--user=$USER`** — limits the cancellation to jobs you own, so other users' jobs are untouched. - **`--partition=<PARTITION_NAME>`** — restricts the scope to the specific partition you name. - **`--state=PENDING`** — targets only jobs that are queued (not yet started). Running jobs (`RUNNING` state) are **not** affected. ### Example To cancel all your pending jobs in the `debug` partition: ```bash scancel --user=$USER --partition=debug --state=PENDING ``` ### Notes - You must have permission to cancel jobs in that partition (typically, you can only cancel your own jobs regardless). - If you want to see what would be cancelled before actually doing it, you can first run: ```bash squeue --user=$USER --partition=<PARTITION_NAME> --state=PENDING ``` to review the list. For more details on `scancel` options, see the [Slurm scancel documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes — filter scancel by state, owner and partition, e.g. `scancel --state=PENDING --user=bob --partition=debug` (use --me instead of --user to mean your own jobs). The -t/--state filter accepts PENDING, RUNNING or SUSPENDED, and when multiple filters are supplied only the jobs satisfying all of the filtering options are signaled, so running jobs in that partition are left alone.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://slurm.schedmd.com/scancel.html

Where on the FASRC Cannon filesystem should I store large datasets that I don't need backed up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-cluster-storage-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For large datasets you don't need backed up, use the **scratch filesystem** at `/n/netscratch` [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). Key points about scratch: - It's **not backed up** — it's intended as volatile, temporary space for transient data - Files older than **90 days will be deleted** through a periodic purge process, which can run at any time (especially if scratch is getting full or during monthly maintenance) - Each lab is allotted **50TB** of scratch space This makes it ideal for large intermediate datasets, temporary working files, or any data you can afford to lose or regenerate. For reference, your home directory (`/home/username`) is regularly backed up but limited to 100GB per user [User Quick Start Guide – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide).
✅ Expected Answer
Use /n/holyscratch01/<lab>/ for fast scratch storage. It is not backed up and is periodically purged of older files.

Question 108: question_108

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed with an out-of-memory error. How do I diagnose what the actual memory peak was, and how do I request more memory for the next submission?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/C/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, slurm.schedmd.com/accounting.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/C/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
# Diagnosing Peak Memory & Requesting More on Cannon ## 1. Diagnose the actual memory peak When SLURM kills a job for exceeding its memory limit, the job state will show `OUT_OF_MEMORY` (or `OUT_OF_ME+` in `sacct` output). To find the **peak memory usage**, use `sstat`: ```bash sstat -j <job_id> --format=MaxRSS,AveRSS,MaxVMSize ``` - **MaxRSS** = peak resident (actual RAM) memory used by the job. - **AveRSS** = average resident memory. - **MaxVMSize** = peak virtual memory. This tells you exactly how much memory your job consumed, so you can size your next request appropriately. ## 2. Request more memory for your next submission SLURM on Cannon offers two ways to request memory: | Flag | What it does | |------|-------------| | `--mem=<total>` | Total memory per **node** (e.g., `--mem=64G`) | | `--mem-per-cpu=<per-core>` | Memory per allocated CPU/core (e.g., `--mem-per-cpu=8G`) | ### Example `sbatch` directives **Option A — memory per node:** ```bash #SBATCH --mem=64G ``` **Option B — memory per CPU:** ```bash #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu=8G ``` *(If you request 8 CPUs with `--cpus-per-task=8`, this gives you 64 GB total.)* ### How to choose - Use `--mem-per-cpu` if your job's memory scales with the number of cores. - Use `--mem` if your job needs a fixed total regardless of core count. - A good starting point is **2–3× the size of your largest data file**, or the peak memory you observed from `sstat` ([Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/)). ## Quick workflow 1. Run `sstat -j <failed_job_id> --format=MaxRSS` to see the peak. 2. Add ~20% headroom and set `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu` accordingly in your next `sbatch` script. 3. Resubmit. If you share your job ID, I can help you interpret the `sstat` output or suggest a concrete memory request.
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,MaxRSS,ReqMem to see the peak memory the job used (MaxRSS) versus what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script, increase the memory request via --mem=<N>G (whole-job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G (per-task), aiming a bit above the observed MaxRSS so future variations don't kill the job again.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's what you need for a multi-node MPI + GPU job on Cannon (1 GPU per node): --- ## 1. SLURM submit script ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH -J my_mpi_gpu_job #SBATCH -N 2 # number of nodes #SBATCH --ntasks-per-node=1 # one MPI rank per node #SBATCH --gpus-per-node=1 # one GPU per node #SBATCH -t 00:30:00 #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -o job.out #SBATCH -e job.err #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu=4G # Load modules module load nvhpc/24.11-fasrc01 # CUDA + NCCL module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 # MPI # Optional NCCL debug # export NCCL_DEBUG=INFO # export NCCL_IB_HCA=mlx5_0 # Launch MPI ranks (one per node, each owning one GPU) srun -n 2 --mpi=pmix ./my_mpi_gpu_program ``` Key points: - **`-N 2`** requests 2 nodes. - **`--ntasks-per-node=1`** gives one MPI rank per node. - **`--gpus-per-node=1`** assigns one GPU to each task. - **`srun -n 2`** launches 2 MPI ranks total (one per node). --- ## 2. Inside your MPI program Each rank gets one GPU. The common pattern is to map the **local rank** to a GPU device: ```c int local_rank = 0; // with 1 GPU per node, always 0 cudaSetDevice(local_rank); ``` Or, if your code supports multiple GPUs per node, use: ```c int local_rank = /* derived from MPI_Comm_rank / MPI_Comm_size */; int device = local_rank % cudaDeviceCount(); cudaSetDevice(device); ``` --- ## 3. PyTorch DDP alternative If you're using PyTorch DDP instead of raw MPI+CUDA, the training example on GitHub shows reading SLURM env vars directly: ```python import os rank = int(os.environ["SLURM_PROCID"]) world_size = int(os.environ["WORLD_SIZE"]) gpus_per_node = int(os.environ["SLURM_GPUS_ON_NODE"]) device = rank % gpus_per_node # with 1 GPU/node → always 0 torch.cuda.set_device(device) ``` The SLURM script sets `WORLD_SIZE=$SLURM_NTASKS`, `MASTER_ADDR`, and `MASTER_PORT` before calling `srun`. See the [Multi-Node Multi-GPU MLP Training with PyTorch DDP](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md) example for a full working script. --- ## 4. NCCL communication For GPU-to-GPU collectives across nodes, use the **NCCL** backend (either via MPI for process orchestration or PMIx). Set `NCCL_IB_HCA=mlx5_0` if using Infiniband, and adjust `NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME` if needed. See the [Distributed GPU Collectives on Multiple Nodes (NCCL + CUDA)](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/NCCL_and_CUDA/MultiNode/README.md) page for full examples. --- Want me to tailor this to your specific code (C/C++ MPI+CUDA, PyTorch, TensorFlow, etc.)?
✅ Expected Answer
In your submit script: choose a GPU-enabled partition (e.g. gpu), set --nodes=<N> with --ntasks-per-node=1 and --gres=gpu:1 (per-node GPU request), load the MPI and CUDA modules with module load. Launch the program with srun (not mpirun directly) so SLURM places one rank per node with the bound GPU; e.g. srun --mpi=pmix ./my_program. Use sinfo and scontrol show partition gpu to confirm node configuration before submitting.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface
